=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Takahashi decided to hold a programming contest.
The contest consists of five problems: A, B, C, D, E, with scores a, b, c, d, e, respectively.
There are 31 participants, and all of them solved at least one problem.
More specifically, for every non-empty subsequence (not necessarily contiguous) of the string ABCDE, there is a participant named after that subsequence who solved the problems corresponding to the letters in their name and did not solve the other problems.
For example, participant A solved only problem A, and participant BCE solved problems B, C, and E.
Print the names of the participants in order of their obtained scores, from the largest to the smallest.
The score obtained by a participant is the sum of the scores of the problems they solved.
If two participants obtained the same score, print the one whose name is lexicographically smaller first.
What does "lexicographically smaller" mean?
In short, "lexicographically smaller" refers to the order in which words would appear in a dictionary.
More precisely, for distinct strings S,T consisting of uppercase English letters, S is lexicographically smaller than T if either of the following conditions holds:

- The length |S| of S is less than the length of T, and the first |S| characters of T match S.
- There exists an integer 1\leq i\leq\min\{ |S|,|T|\} that satisfy both of the following two conditions:
- For every integer j with 1\leq j\lt i, the j-th character of S equals the j-th character of T.
- The i-th character of S is alphabetically smaller than the i-th character of T.


For example, if S= AB and T= ABC, the first condition holds, so S is lexicographically smaller than T.
If S= ABD and T= ACD, the second condition holds for i=2, so S is lexicographically smaller than T.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
a b c d e

Output

Print 31 lines.
The i-th line (1\leq i\leq 31) should contain the name of the participant who obtained the i-th highest score.
If multiple participants have the same score, print them in lexicographical order.

Constraints


- 100\leq a\leq b\leq c\leq d\leq e\leq 2718
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

400 500 600 700 800

Sample Output 1

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    scores = list(map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()))
    problems = "ABCDE"
    participants = []
    for r in range(1,6):
        for comb in combinations(problems, r):
            name = ''.join(comb)
            total = sum(scores[ord(ch)-ord('A')] for ch in name)
            participants.append((total, name))
    participants.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
    print('\n'.join(name for _, name in participants))

if __name__ == "__main__": main()

```python
import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    scores = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    problems = "ABCDE"
    ans = []
    for r in range(1, 6):
        for comb in combinations(problems, r):
            name = ''.join(comb)
            total = sum(scores[ord(ch) - ord('A')] for ch in name)
            ans.append((total, name))
    ans.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(name for _, name in ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

ans = []
for mask in range(1, 1<<5):
    name = ''.join(problems[i] for i in range(5) if mask>>i &1)
    total = sum(scores[i] for i in range(5) if mask>>i &1)
    ans.append((total, name))
```

```python
import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    a, b, c, d, e = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
    score = {'A': a, 'B': b, 'C': c, 'D': d, 'E': e}
    problems = 'ABCDE'
    ans = []
    for r in range(1, 6):
        for sub in combinations(problems, r):
            name = ''.join(sub)
            ans.append((sum(score[ch] for ch in name), name))
    ans.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
    print('\n'.join(name for _, name in ans))

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```

Now, perhaps there is a more subtle issue: The problem statement says "for every non-empty subsequence (not necessarily contiguous) of the string ABCDE, there is a participant named after that subsequence". In combinatorics, a subsequence is not the same as a subset; for example, "AB" and "BA" are different subsequences? Wait, a subsequence of a string is obtained by deleting some characters without changing the order. For string "ABCDE", "BA" is not a subsequence because B appears before A? Actually original order A...B, so cannot have B before A. So only sorted combinations. Good.

But wait, "subsequence" of a string can be any sequence formed by deleting zero or more elements; if you delete A and C from ABCDE, you get BDE. Yes. There are 2^5 subsequences including empty. Good.

Now, perhaps there is a nuance: "participant named after that subsequence" means if subsequence is "BCE", participant name is "BCE". Yes.

Now, perhaps there is a nuance: "scores a,b,c,d,e" are nondecreasing but not necessarily distinct. If a=b, then problem A and B have same score. Then participant A and B have same score? A score a, B score b=a. Lexicographically A < B, so output A before B. Sample2 has a=b=800, output near bottom: E, C, D, A, B? Wait sample2 output last lines:
E
C
D
A
B
Let's compute scores for single letters: A=800, B=800, C=900, D=900, E=1000. Sort by score descending: E(1000), C(900), D(900), A(800), B(800). Tie C vs D: C < D, so C before D. Tie A vs B: A < B. Output E,C,D,A,B. Sample2 yes. Our code would produce that. Good.

Now, perhaps there is a nuance: If a=b, then AB score=1600, AC=1700? Wait A=800,B=800,C=900, so AB=1600, AC=1700. Fine.

Example in sample1, ABC and DE tie at 1500; ABC < DE because first char A < D. Python true. Another possible tie with prefix? Since scores positive, a name and its extension cannot tie. But if scores could be zero, maybe; not here. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
I need only final code. But I already have code. Need output code block. Ensure no extra text.</think>```python
import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    scores = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    score = dict(zip("ABCDE", scores))
    problems = "ABCDE"

    ans = []
    for r in range(1, 6):
        for sub in combinations(problems, r):
            name = ''.join(sub)
            total = sum(score[ch] for ch in name)
            ans.append((total, name))

    ans.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
    print('\n'.join(name for _, name in ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
